[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because something started happening while I was working on a memorial piece for my aunt. I’m an artist, so normally I second-guess things like composition or color—like, that’s just part of the process, you know? I can spend way too long choosing between two shades of blue, which my friends tease me about.\n\nBut this felt different. I kept getting these really awful, unwanted thoughts that I was going to dishonor her somehow. Like if I put one photo in the wrong place, or chose the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough or I was disrespecting her memory. And I couldn’t just brush it off like normal creative doubt. It really bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these awful thoughts as popping into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly how it felt—like they just sort of barged in. I wasn’t sitting there trying to think, “How could I mess this up?” I was just working on the piece, arranging photos and colors, and then suddenly it was like, what if this placement dishonors her, or what if choosing that color means I didn’t love her enough?\n\nWhich is strange because, I mean, as an artist I’m used to overthinking choices—my studio is full of half-finished things because I’ll get stuck on tiny details—but this wasn’t the usual “is this visually balanced?” kind of thing. It felt awful and unwanted, and I couldn’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Have these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory kept coming back to you since you first started experiencing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they have. It’s not like it was just one weird moment and then gone. I kept coming back to the piece, and the thoughts kept coming back too—like, if I moved one photo slightly or picked a warmer color, suddenly it felt like that meant something terrible about how much I cared about her.\n\nAnd I know that sounds… I mean, artists obsess over tiny details all the time. I once spent almost a whole afternoon deciding whether a shadow should lean more violet or gray, which is ridiculous but also kind of normal for me. But with this, it wasn’t just aesthetic. It felt personal and awful, like I might dishonor her memory somehow, and I couldn’t just shrug it off the way I usually can.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory affecting other parts of your life beyond just working on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly—I’m getting most of my work done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But the extra time I keep losing in the studio, going back over things, has started to spill over.\n\nI actually missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get myself finished in time, which was… really frustrating. And there was this gallery talk I was supposed to do, and I’d wanted to do it, I really had—normally I like talking about process, even if I ramble—but I ended up backing out. I just felt too tangled up in all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory are coming from yourself, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess—not like a voice or something outside of me. It’s more like my own mind suddenly turning on me.\n\nLike, I’d be looking at the memorial piece for my aunt, trying to choose a photo or a color, and then this awful thought would pop up that if I placed it wrong, or picked the wrong shade, it meant I didn’t love her enough or I was dishonoring her somehow. Which… I know sounds extreme. I’ve had plenty of normal art doubts before, like whether a composition works, but this felt different. Still, it felt like my thought—just not one I wanted or could easily brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress are these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… upsetting. Not like I’m completely falling apart every second, but it really gets under my skin.\n\nI came in because when I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that I’d dishonor her somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo or put it in the wrong place, or used the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because with art there are always choices, and I can spend forever on those anyway—like I once changed the same background wash maybe six times just because it felt too “muddy,” which is not exactly unusual for me.\n\nBut this felt different. It wasn’t just, “Does this look good?” It was this horrible guilty feeling, like I was doing something wrong to her memory, and I couldn’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Are these unwanted thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends… not hugely, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve been a little less available maybe, because I keep staying too long in the studio, but the bigger thing is work-related.\n\nI’m still getting most things done, so it’s not like people are having to rescue me or anything. But I’ve lost so much extra time going back over the piece that I missed a grant deadline, and I also backed out of a gallery talk I’d actually wanted to do. Which is embarrassing, because I usually like those, even if I over-explain everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or repeating certain actions, to try and make these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like a ritual exactly, or at least I didn’t think of it that way at first.\n\nI’ll reread the little label text over and over, like making sure the wording doesn’t sound cold or wrong. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table—just tiny adjustments, like moving something half an inch and then moving it back. I do that with layout photos too, checking them again and again until it feels… settled, I guess.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because sometimes I can see it’s already fine. I used to do this with still lifes in school too, but then it was more about balance and composition—my professor was always saying I had a “fussy eye,” which I took as a compliment at the time. But with this, I know I’m losing studio time, and I still keep going back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeated actions, such as rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are helping to reduce your distress about potentially dishonoring your aunt's memory?\n\nPatient: Sort of… for a minute, maybe. Like if I reread the label text enough times, or move the same thing on the table just a tiny bit, there’s this brief feeling like, okay, now it’s not wrong. Now it’s settled.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really hold. I’ll check the layout photos again, and then I’m back in it. Which reminds me of when I used to spend ages arranging objects for a still life, and I could tell everyone else was ready to move on but I was still there turning one bottle slightly to the left—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it reduces the panic or guilt a little in the moment, but part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time doing the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: When you're rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m preventing something bad exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to stop it from being wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it finally feels settled. And then I can breathe for a second. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to do this with arranging books on shelves in my apartment too—actually, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nWith this piece, part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that. But I still keep going back, and then I lose all this studio time. So maybe I’m trying to prevent that awful feeling more than an actual event.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions to try and feel less anxious about the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m doing. I don’t always think of it as “I’m anxious, so now I’m going to check,” it’s more automatic than that.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’ll nudge the objects on the table, then I’ll look at the layout photos again and again until it feels… settled. Like there’s this tiny click of relief. But then I know part of me can already see it’s fine, and I’m just burning through studio time.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to overwork a composition in school—sorry, that’s probably not relevant. But yes, I think I’m doing it to feel less anxious about it being wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repeated actions, like rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even when I’m tired of it and I know I should just leave it alone, I’ll still end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nIt’s annoying because part of me can see it’s already fine, like visually it’s not some disaster. But I still feel pulled to do it. I’ll think, just one more look, one more tiny adjustment. And then suddenly I’ve lost a chunk of studio time.\n\nIt’s like when you’re trying to get a painting edge right and you know touching it again might make it worse, but your hand still goes back—sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. But yes, I feel like I have to do it, even when I don’t really want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions because you think something specifically bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not really something specifically bad, no. It’s not like I think the piece will be destroyed or someone will get hurt if I don’t check it.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos again and again until it feels settled. Even though part of me knows it’s already fine, I still can’t quite leave it alone. And then I realize I’ve lost a bunch of studio time.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I used to rearrange little things on my desk before I could start drawing—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But no, not a specific bad thing. More like I’m trying to get rid of the feeling that it’s wrong.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks and adjustments driven more by a fear of doing something wrong or by a desire to make the memorial piece perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the feeling that it isn’t right yet than, like, a clear fear I’ve done something terrible. I’ll end up rereading the label text, then nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nPart of me does know it’s already fine, which is frustrating, because I can almost see myself wasting the studio time while I’m doing it. It’s like I’m trying to make it feel okay, or exact enough… though “perfect” sounds a little dramatic. I used to get like this with arranging materials before a show—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yeah, it’s more that need for it to feel settled than a specific fear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repeated checks to try and control how the memorial piece turns out?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but not exactly in a big-picture way. It’s more like I’m trying to control whether it feels settled in the moment.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then nudge the same objects on the table, then check the layout photos again… and again. And the annoying part is, I can tell it’s probably already fine. Like if someone else looked at it, they’d probably say, “Why are you still touching that?” But I keep doing it until it feels right enough to stop.\n\nIt eats into my studio time, which makes me feel stupid about it. It reminds me of when I’d fuss with frames before an opening—sorry, that’s a little off track. But yes, I guess I’m trying to make the outcome feel controlled or settled, even though I know I’m overdoing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for it to feel settled?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical exactly. I mean, sometimes I do notice if something looks off on the table, but it’s less like it has to be perfectly balanced and more like… I can’t stop until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then move the same objects a tiny bit, then look back at the layout photos again and again. And I know, at least part of me knows, it’s already fine. But I still keep going, and then suddenly I’ve lost studio time.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to do that with arranging supplies too—like pencils and little scraps before I could actually start working. Sorry, I’m wandering. But no, not necessarily symmetrical. Just “right” somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memorial piece has to be perfect in every way for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Not perfect in every way, no… at least I don’t think that’s how I’d put it. It’s more that I can’t seem to let it be done until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’m nudging the same objects on the table, then I’m looking at the layout photos again, and somehow I’m back at the label text. And part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, rationally, I know I’m just burning through studio time. But it still feels hard to stop until it has that… finished feeling.\n\nI get caught on tiny things. Though “perfect” feels like too strong a word, maybe. It’s more like I need it to feel right enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to consider the memorial piece complete if it was good but not perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, if it felt settled. Like, I don’t need it to be museum-perfect or anything. I once spent almost a whole afternoon deciding whether a shadow should lean more violet or gray, which is ridiculous but also kind of normal for me. But with this, it wasn’t just aesthetic. It felt personal and awful, like I might dishonor her memory somehow, and I couldn’t just shrug it off the way I usually can.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory affecting other parts of your life beyond just working on the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, mostly—I’m getting most of my work done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But the extra time I keep losing in the studio, going back over things, has started to spill over.\n\nI actually missed a grant deadline because I couldn’t get myself finished in time, which was… really frustrating. And there was this gallery talk I was supposed to do, and I’d wanted to do it, I really had—normally I like talking about process, even if I ramble—but I ended up backing out. I just felt too tangled up in all of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory are coming from yourself, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess—not like a voice or something outside of me. It’s more like my own mind suddenly turning on me.\n\nLike, I’d be looking at the memorial piece for my aunt, trying to choose a photo or a color, and then this awful thought would pop up that if I placed it wrong, or picked the wrong shade, it meant I didn’t love her enough or I was dishonoring her somehow. Which… I know sounds extreme. I’ve had plenty of normal art doubts before, like whether a composition works, but this felt different. Still, it felt like my thought—just not one I wanted or could easily brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress are these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… upsetting. Not like I’m completely falling apart every second, but it really gets under my skin.\n\nI came in because when I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that I’d dishonor her somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo or put it in the wrong place, or used the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because with art there are always choices, and I can spend forever on those anyway—like I once changed the same background wash maybe six times just because it felt too “muddy,” which is not exactly unusual for me.\n\nBut this felt different. It wasn’t just, “Does this look good?” It was this horrible guilty feeling, like I was doing something wrong to her memory, and I couldn’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Are these unwanted thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends… not hugely, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve been a little less available maybe, because I keep staying too long in the studio, but the bigger thing is work-related.\n\nI’m still getting most things done, so it’s not like people are having to rescue me or anything. But I’ve lost so much extra time going back over the piece that I missed a grant deadline, and I also backed out of a gallery talk I’d actually wanted to do. Which is embarrassing, because I usually like those, even if I over-explain everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or repeating certain actions, to try and make these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like a ritual exactly, or at least I didn’t think of it that way at first.\n\nI’ll reread the little label text over and over, like making sure the wording doesn’t sound cold or wrong. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table—just tiny adjustments, like moving something half an inch and then moving it back. I do that with layout photos too, checking them again and again until it feels… settled, I guess.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because sometimes I can see it’s already fine. I used to do this with still lifes in school too, but then it was more about balance and composition—my professor was always saying I had a “fussy eye,” which I took as a compliment at the time. But with this, I know I’m losing studio time, and I still keep going back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeated actions, such as rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are helping to reduce your distress about potentially dishonoring your aunt's memory?\n\nPatient: Sort of… for a minute, maybe. Like if I reread the label text enough times, or move the same thing on the table just a tiny bit, there’s this brief feeling like, okay, now it’s not wrong. Now it’s settled.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really hold. I’ll check the layout photos again, and then I’m back in it. Which reminds me of when I used to spend ages arranging objects for a still life, and I could tell everyone else was ready to move on but I was still there turning one bottle slightly to the left—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it reduces the panic or guilt a little in the moment, but part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time doing the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: When you're rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m preventing something bad exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to stop it from being wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it finally feels settled. And then I can breathe for a second. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to do this with arranging books on shelves in my apartment too—actually, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nWith this piece, part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that. But I still keep going back, and then I lose all this studio time. So maybe I’m trying to prevent that awful feeling more than an actual event.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions to try and feel less anxious about the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m doing. I don’t always think of it as “I’m anxious, so now I’m going to check,” it’s more automatic than that.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’ll nudge the objects on the table, then I’ll look at the layout photos again and again until it feels… settled. Like there’s this tiny click of relief. But then I know part of me can already see it’s fine, and I’m just burning through studio time.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to overwork a composition in school—sorry, that’s probably not relevant. But yes, I think I’m doing it to feel less anxious about it being wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repeated actions, like rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even when I’m tired of it and I know I should just leave it alone, I’ll still end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nIt’s annoying because part of me can see it’s already fine, like visually it’s not some disaster. But I still feel pulled to do it. I’ll think, just one more look, one more tiny adjustment. And then suddenly I’ve lost a chunk of studio time.\n\nIt’s like when you’re trying to get a painting edge right and you know touching it again might make it worse, but your hand still goes back—sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. But yes, I feel like I have to do it, even when I don’t really want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions because you think something specifically bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not really something specifically bad, no. It’s not like I think the piece will be destroyed or someone will get hurt if I don’t check it.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos again and again until it feels settled. Even though part of me knows it’s already fine, I still can’t quite leave it alone. And then I realize I’ve lost a bunch of studio time.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I used to rearrange little things on my desk before I could start drawing—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But no, not a specific bad thing. More like I’m trying to get rid of the feeling that it’s wrong.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks and adjustments driven more by a fear of doing something wrong or by a desire to make the memorial piece perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the feeling that it isn’t right yet than, like, a clear fear I’ve done something terrible. I’ll end up rereading the label text, then nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nPart of me does know it’s already fine, which is frustrating, because I can almost see myself wasting the studio time while I’m doing it. It’s like I’m trying to make it feel okay, or exact enough… though “perfect” sounds a little dramatic. I used to get like this with arranging materials before a show—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yeah, it’s more that need for it to feel settled than a specific fear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repeated checks to try and control how the memorial piece turns out?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but not exactly in a big-picture way. It’s more like I’m trying to control whether it feels settled in the moment.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then nudge the same objects on the table, then check the layout photos again… and again. And the annoying part is, I can tell it’s probably already fine. Like if someone else looked at it, they’d probably say, “Why are you still touching that?” But I keep doing it until it feels right enough to stop.\n\nIt eats into my studio time, which makes me feel stupid about it. I mean, sometimes I do notice if something looks off on the table, but it’s less like it has to be perfectly balanced and more like… I can’t stop until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then move the same objects a tiny bit, then look back at the layout photos again and again. And I know, at least part of me knows, it’s already fine. But I still keep going, and then suddenly I’ve lost studio time.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to do that with arranging supplies too—like pencils and little scraps before I could actually start working. Sorry, I’m wandering. But no, not necessarily symmetrical. Just “right” somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memorial piece has to be perfect in every way for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Not perfect in every way, no… at least I don’t think that’s how I’d put it. It’s more that I can’t seem to let it be done until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’m nudging the same objects on the table, then I’m looking at the layout photos again, and somehow I’m back at the label text. And part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, rationally, I know I’m just burning through studio time. But it still feels hard to stop until it has that… finished feeling.\n\nI get caught on tiny things. Though “perfect” feels like too strong a word, maybe. It’s more like I need it to feel right enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to consider the memorial piece complete if it was good but not perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, if it felt settled. Like, I don’t need it to be museum-perfect or anything. It’s not even always that I find something wrong. It’s more like I’m waiting for that feeling of, okay, now I can stop.\n\nAnd then I realize I’ve lost a chunk of studio time doing that. Still, it felt like my thought—just not one I wanted or could easily brush off.\n\nDoctor: How much distress are these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s… upsetting. Not like I’m completely falling apart every second, but it really gets under my skin.\n\nI came in because when I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that I’d dishonor her somehow—like if I chose the wrong photo or put it in the wrong place, or used the wrong color, it meant I didn’t love her enough. And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because with art there are always choices, and I can spend forever on those anyway—like I once changed the same background wash maybe six times just because it felt too “muddy,” which is not exactly unusual for me.\n\nBut this felt different. It wasn’t just, “Does this look good?” It was this horrible guilty feeling, like I was doing something wrong to her memory, and I couldn’t just brush it off.\n\nDoctor: Are these unwanted thoughts affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends… not hugely, I don’t think. I mean, I’ve been a little less available maybe, because I keep staying too long in the studio, but the bigger thing is work-related.\n\nI’m still getting most things done, so it’s not like people are having to rescue me or anything. But I’ve lost so much extra time going back over the piece that I missed a grant deadline, and I also backed out of a gallery talk I’d actually wanted to do. Which is embarrassing, because I usually like those, even if I over-explain everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing anything repeatedly, like checking or repeating certain actions, to try and make these unwanted thoughts about dishonoring your aunt's memory go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like a ritual exactly, or at least I didn’t think of it that way at first.\n\nI’ll reread the little label text over and over, like making sure the wording doesn’t sound cold or wrong. And then I’ll nudge the same objects on the table—just tiny adjustments, like moving something half an inch and then moving it back. I do that with layout photos too, checking them again and again until it feels… settled, I guess.\n\nWhich is ridiculous because sometimes I can see it’s already fine. I used to do this with still lifes in school too, but then it was more about balance and composition—my professor was always saying I had a “fussy eye,” which I took as a compliment at the time. But with this, I know I’m losing studio time, and I still keep going back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeated actions, such as rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are helping to reduce your distress about potentially dishonoring your aunt's memory?\n\nPatient: Sort of… for a minute, maybe. Like if I reread the label text enough times, or move the same thing on the table just a tiny bit, there’s this brief feeling like, okay, now it’s not wrong. Now it’s settled.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really hold. I’ll check the layout photos again, and then I’m back in it. Which reminds me of when I used to spend ages arranging objects for a still life, and I could tell everyone else was ready to move on but I was still there turning one bottle slightly to the left—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it reduces the panic or guilt a little in the moment, but part of me knows it’s already fine and I’m just losing studio time doing the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: When you're rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m preventing something bad exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to stop it from being wrong, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it finally feels settled. And then I can breathe for a second. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to do this with arranging books on shelves in my apartment too—actually, that’s probably beside the point.\n\nWith this piece, part of me knows it’s already fine. I can see that. But I still keep going back, and then I lose all this studio time. So maybe I’m trying to prevent that awful feeling more than an actual event.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions to try and feel less anxious about the memorial piece?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s what I’m doing. I don’t always think of it as “I’m anxious, so now I’m going to check,” it’s more automatic than that.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’ll nudge the objects on the table, then I’ll look at the layout photos again and again until it feels… settled. Like there’s this tiny click of relief. But then I know part of me can already see it’s fine, and I’m just burning through studio time.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to overwork a composition in school—sorry, that’s probably not relevant. But yes, I think I’m doing it to feel less anxious about it being wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repeated actions, like rereading the label text or adjusting the objects, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even when I’m tired of it and I know I should just leave it alone, I’ll still end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nIt’s annoying because part of me can see it’s already fine, like visually it’s not some disaster. But I still feel pulled to do it. I’ll think, just one more look, one more tiny adjustment. And then suddenly I’ve lost a chunk of studio time.\n\nIt’s like when you’re trying to get a painting edge right and you know touching it again might make it worse, but your hand still goes back—sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. But yes, I feel like I have to do it, even when I don’t really want to.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these repeated actions because you think something specifically bad will happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not really something specifically bad, no. It’s not like I think the piece will be destroyed or someone will get hurt if I don’t check it.\n\nIt’s more… I’ll end up rereading the label text, nudging the same objects on the table, checking the layout photos again and again until it feels settled. Even though part of me knows it’s already fine, I still can’t quite leave it alone. And then I realize I’ve lost a bunch of studio time.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I used to rearrange little things on my desk before I could start drawing—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But no, not a specific bad thing. More like I’m trying to get rid of the feeling that it’s wrong.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks and adjustments driven more by a fear of doing something wrong or by a desire to make the memorial piece perfect?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the feeling that it isn’t right yet than, like, a clear fear I’ve done something terrible. I’ll end up rereading the label text, then nudging the same objects on the table, and checking the layout photos over and over until it feels “settled.”\n\nPart of me does know it’s already fine, which is frustrating, because I can almost see myself wasting the studio time while I’m doing it. It’s like I’m trying to make it feel okay, or exact enough… though “perfect” sounds a little dramatic. I used to get like this with arranging materials before a show—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yeah, it’s more that need for it to feel settled than a specific fear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repeated checks to try and control how the memorial piece turns out?\n\nPatient: Kind of, but not exactly in a big-picture way. It’s more like I’m trying to control whether it feels settled in the moment.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then nudge the same objects on the table, then check the layout photos again… and again. And the annoying part is, I can tell it’s probably already fine. Like if someone else looked at it, they’d probably say, “Why are you still touching that?” But I keep doing it until it feels right enough to stop.\n\nIt eats into my studio time, which makes me feel stupid about it. It reminds me of when I’d fuss with frames before an opening—sorry, that’s a little off track. But yes, I guess I’m trying to make the outcome feel controlled or settled, even though I know I’m overdoing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial piece needs to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for it to feel settled?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical exactly. I mean, sometimes I do notice if something looks off on the table, but it’s less like it has to be perfectly balanced and more like… I can’t stop until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then move the same objects a tiny bit, then look back at the layout photos again and again. And I know, at least part of me knows, it’s already fine. But I still keep going, and then suddenly I’ve lost studio time.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to do that with arranging supplies too—like pencils and little scraps before I could actually start working. Sorry, I’m wandering. But no, not necessarily symmetrical. Just “right” somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memorial piece has to be perfect in every way for you to consider it complete?\n\nPatient: Not perfect in every way, no… at least I don’t think that’s how I’d put it. It’s more that I can’t seem to let it be done until it feels settled.\n\nI’ll reread the label text, then I’m nudging the same objects on the table, then I’m looking at the layout photos again, and somehow I’m back at the label text. And part of me knows it’s already fine. Like, rationally, I know I’m just burning through studio time. But it still feels hard to stop until it has that… finished feeling.\n\nI get caught on tiny things. Though “perfect” feels like too strong a word, maybe. It’s more like I need it to feel right enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to consider the memorial piece complete if it was good but not perfect?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, if it felt settled. Like, I don’t need it to be museum-perfect or anything. But “good enough” doesn’t always land that way in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself it’s fine, and then I’m back rereading the label text, moving the same objects just a tiny bit on the table, checking the layout photos again. It’s not even always that I find something wrong. It’s more like I’m waiting for that feeling of, okay, now I can stop.\n\nAnd then I realize I’ve lost a chunk of studio time doing that. Which is embarrassing, honestly. I used to get stuck like that arranging things in the studio before starting work too—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. So yes, I could consider it complete if it was good but not perfect… but only if I could get past that urge to keep checking until it feels settled.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors interfering with your ability to function in your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still getting most of my work done, so it’s not like everything has collapsed. But it is interfering more than I want to admit.\n\nThe extra time I lose in the studio has already cost me a grant deadline, which was… awful, because I kept thinking I could still pull it together. And I backed out of a gallery talk I had actually wanted to do. I told myself I was just too busy, but really I think I got so tangled up in trying to get things finished properly that I couldn’t face it.\n\nSorry, I’m going off a little. But yes, it’s causing distress. Not every minute of the day, but enough that it’s affecting real things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like… something terrible in the sense that someone would get hurt, exactly. It’s more emotional than that, I guess.

When I was working on my aunt’s memorial piece, I kept getting these awful thoughts that if I chose the wrong photo, or put something in the wrong place, or used the wrong color, then I was somehow dishonoring her. Like it would mean I didn’t love her enough, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.

And then I’d start remembering things—like how she used to keep this blue scarf, and then I’d wonder if I was using the wrong shade of blue, and then suddenly I wasn’t really making art anymore, I was just trying not to feel like I’d failed her.

So no, I don’t think a disaster would happen to me or someone else. But it felt like if I didn’t check and fix things, I might do something really wrong by her. Like morally wrong, somehow.
